And, like I said about the newsletter, I've definitely started ducking out of the way when I see a camera. I even find myself hesitating a bit before going to some of the work get-togethers or social things that I used to just go to without thinking about it. It just makes me feel a little more self-conscious, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, as a way to deal with these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. Before I have to do a safety class, or even if I'm just heading to a senior-center workshop, I’ll go check the bathroom mirror a few times. I'll put lotion on my hands, and I kind of turn my face different ways in the light just to see how it looks. \n\nSometimes I'll even ask a coworker if I look all right, just to be sure. Doing all that does make me run a little behind schedule sometimes, like I mentioned earlier, but... I still get myself there. I don't let it stop me from showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after checking your appearance in the mirror or getting reassurance from a coworker?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it like that. \n\nI guess I just feel like I need to check, but... I don't know if I actually feel any better or less anxious afterward. Even if a coworker says I look fine, I... I don't really know if it changes how I feel about it. I just sort of do it and then go get on with my shift.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Doing all that does make me run a little behind schedule sometimes, like I mentioned earlier, but... I still get myself there. I'll put lotion on my hands, and I kind of turn my face different ways in the light just to see how it looks. \n\nSometimes I'll even ask a coworker if I look all right, just to be sure. Doing all that does make me run a little behind schedule sometimes, like I mentioned earlier, but... I still get myself there. I don't let it stop me from showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after checking your appearance in the mirror or getting reassurance from a coworker?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it like that. \n\nI guess I just feel like I need to check, but... I don't know if I actually feel any better or less anxious afterward. Even if a coworker says I look fine, I... I don't really know if it changes how I feel about it. I just sort of do it and then go get on with my shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror, or is it something you just do because you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't really say I want to do it. It's certainly not something I enjoy doing. But... I don't know if I'd say I absolutely have to, either. \n\nI guess it just feels like something I need to get out of the way before I go stand in front of people for a training. I just feel this pull to go check, so I do. I haven't really broken it down like that before, to be honest. It's just become part of my routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that there might be something medically wrong with your hands or face, like a disease or a defect?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's a medical problem. I've never worried about it being a disease or anything like that. I just... I just don't like how they look. It's not a health issue, just an appearance thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hands and face are actually flawed in some way, or are you more concerned about what other people might think of how you look?\n\nPatient: Well, I really do think they're flawed. It's actually why I came in. I recently started helping out with the safety trainings at the plant, and... I just can't seem to stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look when I'm up there pointing things out to people. \n\nAnd then with my face, whenever I see myself in the company newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked. Other people tell me they don't see it at all, but I definitely do. So, yeah, I do think there's actually something wrong with how they look, even if other people say they don't notice it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Like, when I’m pointing things out to the others, all I can focus on is how wrinkly and uneven my hands look. \n\nAnd then... well, it's my face, too. They took some photos of the trainings for the company newsletter, and looking at them, I just keep thinking my face looks crooked. I've pointed it out, but other people say they don’t see it at all. It just makes me feel a little more self-conscious, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, as a way to deal with these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. Before I have to do a safety class, or even if I'm just heading to a senior-center workshop, I’ll go check the bathroom mirror a few times. I'll put lotion on my hands, and I kind of turn my face different ways in the light just to see how it looks. \n\nSometimes I'll even ask a coworker if I look all right, just to be sure. Doing all that does make me run a little behind schedule sometimes, like I mentioned earlier, but... I still get myself there. I don't let it stop me from showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after checking your appearance in the mirror or getting reassurance from a coworker?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it like that. \n\nI guess I just feel like I need to check, but... I don't know if I actually feel any better or less anxious afterward. Even if a coworker says I look fine, I... I don't really know if it changes how I feel about it. I just sort of do it and then go get on with my shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror, or is it something you just do because you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't really say I want to do it. It's certainly not something I enjoy doing. But... I don't know if I'd say I absolutely have to, either. \n\nI guess it just feels like something I need to get out of the way before I go stand in front of people for a training. I just feel this pull to go check, so I do. I haven't really broken it down like that before, to be honest. It's just become part of my routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that there might be something medically wrong with your hands or face, like a disease or a defect?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's a medical problem. I've never worried about it being a disease or anything like that. I just... I just don't like how they look. It's not a health issue, just an appearance thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hands and face are actually flawed in some way, or are you more concerned about what other people might think of how you look?\n\nPatient: Well, I really do think they're flawed. It's actually why I came in. I recently started helping out with the safety trainings at the plant, and... I just can't seem to stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look when I'm up there pointing things out to people. \n\nAnd then with my face, whenever I see myself in the company newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked. Other people tell me they don't see it at all, but I definitely do. So, yeah, I do think there's actually something wrong with how they look, even if other people say they don't notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be bothered by the appearance of your hands and face if you were alone and nobody else could see you?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest. I haven't thought about it like that. \n\nI mean, I mostly get bothered when I'm up doing those safety trainings or looking at the company newsletter. If I was just home alone and knew I didn't have to see anyone... I don't know. I still don't like how they look when I'm looking in my own mirror at the house, but I couldn't say for sure if it would weigh on me the exact same way. I just don't have a good answer for that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about the appearance of your hands and face if nobody else could see you?\n\nPatient: Like I just said, I really couldn't tell you. I haven't ever thought about it that way. I mean, I still see it when I look in the mirror at home, but I don't know if I'd care as much if I never had to go out and see people. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still think about your hands and face as much if you didn't have to interact with other people at work?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know how to answer that. I mean, it's just hard to say. The work trainings and the newsletters are what really set it off right now, but... if I didn't have to do those, or interact with people? I'm not sure if it would still be on my mind as much. I just haven't been in a situation like that to really know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

They took some photos of the trainings for the company newsletter, and looking at them, I just keep thinking my face looks crooked. I've pointed it out, but other people say they don’t see it at all. It's just really been bothering me, and I can't seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts about your hands and face affecting your work or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped me from doing my job or anything like that. I still go to work and I still do the safety trainings. But... I guess I am a bit slower getting ready for work on the days I have to do the trainings, just trying to make sure I look okay. And, like I said about the newsletter, I've definitely started ducking out of the way when I see a camera. I even find myself hesitating a bit before going to some of the work get-togethers or social things that I used to just go to without thinking about it. It just makes me feel a little more self-conscious, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, as a way to deal with these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. Before I have to do a safety class, or even if I'm just heading to a senior-center workshop, I’ll go check the bathroom mirror a few times. I'll put lotion on my hands, and I kind of turn my face different ways in the light just to see how it looks. \n\nSometimes I'll even ask a coworker if I look all right, just to be sure. Doing all that does make me run a little behind schedule sometimes, like I mentioned earlier, but... I still get myself there. I don't let it stop me from showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after checking your appearance in the mirror or getting reassurance from a coworker?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it like that. \n\nI guess I just feel like I need to check, but... I don't know if I actually feel any better or less anxious afterward. Even if a coworker says I look fine, I... I don't really know if it changes how I feel about it. I just sort of do it and then go get on with my shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror, or is it something you just do because you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't really say I want to do it. It's certainly not something I enjoy doing. But... I don't know if I'd say I absolutely have to, either. \n\nI guess it just feels like something I need to get out of the way before I go stand in front of people for a training. I just feel this pull to go check, so I do. I haven't really broken it down like that before, to be honest. It's just become part of my routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that there might be something medically wrong with your hands or face, like a disease or a defect?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's a medical problem. I've never worried about it being a disease or anything like that. I just... I just don't like how they look. It's not a health issue, just an appearance thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hands and face are actually flawed in some way, or are you more concerned about what other people might think of how you look?\n\nPatient: Well, I really do think they're flawed. It's actually why I came in. I recently started helping out with the safety trainings at the plant, and... I just can't seem to stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look when I'm up there pointing things out to people. \n\nAnd then with my face, whenever I see myself in the company newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked. Other people tell me they don't see it at all, but I definitely do. So, yeah, I do think there's actually something wrong with how they look, even if other people say they don't notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be bothered by the appearance of your hands and face if you were alone and nobody else could see you?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest. I haven't thought about it like that. \n\nI mean, I mostly get bothered when I'm up doing those safety trainings or looking at the company newsletter. If I was just home alone and knew I didn't have to see anyone... I don't know. I still don't like how they look when I'm looking in my own mirror at the house, but I couldn't say for sure if it would weigh on me the exact same way. I just don't have a good answer for that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about the appearance of your hands and face if nobody else could see you?\n\nPatient: Like I just said, I really couldn't tell you. I haven't ever thought about it that way. I mean, I still see it when I look in the mirror at home, but I don't know if I'd care as much if I never had to go out and see people. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still think about your hands and face as much if you didn't have to interact with other people at work?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know how to answer that. I mean, it's just hard to say. The work trainings and the newsletters are what really set it off right now, but... if I didn't have to do those, or interact with people? I'm not sure if it would still be on my mind as much. I just haven't been in a situation like that to really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about how your hands and face look if you were in a situation where you knew nobody was judging you?\n\nPatient: I really don't know what else to tell you about that. I mean, it's just hard for me to imagine a situation where I wouldn't feel judged, you know? So I can't really say if I'd still be worried or not. I just... I don't know the answer to that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I came in because... I guess I've been getting really stuck on some things lately. I work in production, and they recently had me start helping out with the safety trainings. But ever since I started doing that, I just can’t seem to stop noticing my hands. Like, when I’m pointing things out to the others, all I can focus on is how wrinkly and uneven my hands look. \n\nAnd then... well, it's my face, too. They took some photos of the trainings for the company newsletter, and looking at them, I just keep thinking my face looks crooked. I've pointed it out, but other people say they don’t see it at all. It's just really been bothering me, and I can't seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts about your hands and face affecting your work or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped me from doing my job or anything like that. I still go to work and I still do the safety trainings. But... I guess I am a bit slower getting ready for work on the days I have to do the trainings, just trying to make sure I look okay. And, like I said about the newsletter, I've definitely started ducking out of the way when I see a camera. I even find myself hesitating a bit before going to some of the work get-togethers or social things that I used to just go to without thinking about it. It just makes me feel a little more self-conscious, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, as a way to deal with these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. Before I have to do a safety class, or even if I'm just heading to a senior-center workshop, I’ll go check the bathroom mirror a few times. I'll put lotion on my hands, and I kind of turn my face different ways in the light just to see how it looks. \n\nSometimes I'll even ask a coworker if I look all right, just to be sure. Doing all that does make me run a little behind schedule sometimes, like I mentioned earlier, but... I still get myself there. I don't let it stop me from showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after checking your appearance in the mirror or getting reassurance from a coworker?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it like that. \n\nI guess I just feel like I need to check, but... I don't know if I actually feel any better or less anxious afterward. Even if a coworker says I look fine, I... I don't really know if it changes how I feel about it. I just sort of do it and then go get on with my shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror, or is it something you just do because you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't really say I want to do it. It's certainly not something I enjoy doing. But... I don't know if I'd say I absolutely have to, either. \n\nI guess it just feels like something I need to get out of the way before I go stand in front of people for a training. I just feel this pull to go check, so I do. I haven't really broken it down like that before, to be honest. It's just become part of my routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that there might be something medically wrong with your hands or face, like a disease or a defect?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's a medical problem. I've never worried about it being a disease or anything like that. I just... I just don't like how they look. It's not a health issue, just an appearance thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hands and face are actually flawed in some way, or are you more concerned about what other people might think of how you look?\n\nPatient: Well, I really do think they're flawed. It's actually why I came in. I recently started helping out with the safety trainings at the plant, and... I just can't seem to stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look when I'm up there pointing things out to people. \n\nAnd then with my face, whenever I see myself in the company newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked. Other people tell me they don't see it at all, but I definitely do. So, yeah, I do think there's actually something wrong with how they look, even if other people say they don't notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be bothered by the appearance of your hands and face if you were alone and nobody else could see you?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest. Like, when I’m pointing things out to the others, all I can focus on is how wrinkly and uneven my hands look. \n\nAnd then... well, it's my face, too. They took some photos of the trainings for the company newsletter, and looking at them, I just keep thinking my face looks crooked. I've pointed it out, but other people say they don’t see it at all. It's just really been bothering me, and I can't seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts about your hands and face affecting your work or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped me from doing my job or anything like that. I still go to work and I still do the safety trainings. But... I guess I am a bit slower getting ready for work on the days I have to do the trainings, just trying to make sure I look okay. And, like I said about the newsletter, I've definitely started ducking out of the way when I see a camera. I even find myself hesitating a bit before going to some of the work get-togethers or social things that I used to just go to without thinking about it. It just makes me feel a little more self-conscious, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, as a way to deal with these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. Before I have to do a safety class, or even if I'm just heading to a senior-center workshop, I’ll go check the bathroom mirror a few times. I'll put lotion on my hands, and I kind of turn my face different ways in the light just to see how it looks. \n\nSometimes I'll even ask a coworker if I look all right, just to be sure. Doing all that does make me run a little behind schedule sometimes, like I mentioned earlier, but... I still get myself there. I don't let it stop me from showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after checking your appearance in the mirror or getting reassurance from a coworker?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it like that. \n\nI guess I just feel like I need to check, but... I don't know if I actually feel any better or less anxious afterward. Even if a coworker says I look fine, I... I don't really know if it changes how I feel about it. I just sort of do it and then go get on with my shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror, or is it something you just do because you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't really say I want to do it. It's certainly not something I enjoy doing. But... Other people tell me they don't see it at all, but I definitely do. I can't really separate the two, to be honest.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance if you were certain that nobody would be looking at you or evaluating how you look?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / production_worker / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. I work in production, and they recently had me start helping out with the safety trainings. But ever since I started doing that, I just can’t seem to stop noticing my hands. Like, when I’m pointing things out to the others, all I can focus on is how wrinkly and uneven my hands look. \n\nAnd then... well, it's my face, too. They took some photos of the trainings for the company newsletter, and looking at them, I just keep thinking my face looks crooked. I've pointed it out, but other people say they don’t see it at all. It's just really been bothering me, and I can't seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts about your hands and face affecting your work or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped me from doing my job or anything like that. I still go to work and I still do the safety trainings. But... I guess I am a bit slower getting ready for work on the days I have to do the trainings, just trying to make sure I look okay. And, like I said about the newsletter, I've definitely started ducking out of the way when I see a camera. I even find myself hesitating a bit before going to some of the work get-togethers or social things that I used to just go to without thinking about it. It just makes me feel a little more self-conscious, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, as a way to deal with these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. Before I have to do a safety class, or even if I'm just heading to a senior-center workshop, I’ll go check the bathroom mirror a few times. I'll put lotion on my hands, and I kind of turn my face different ways in the light just to see how it looks. \n\nSometimes I'll even ask a coworker if I look all right, just to be sure. Doing all that does make me run a little behind schedule sometimes, like I mentioned earlier, but... I still get myself there. I don't let it stop me from showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after checking your appearance in the mirror or getting reassurance from a coworker?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it like that. \n\nI guess I just feel like I need to check, but... I don't know if I actually feel any better or less anxious afterward. Even if a coworker says I look fine, I... I don't really know if it changes how I feel about it. I just sort of do it and then go get on with my shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror, or is it something you just do because you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't really say I want to do it. It's certainly not something I enjoy doing. But... I don't know if I'd say I absolutely have to, either. \n\nI guess it just feels like something I need to get out of the way before I go stand in front of people for a training. I just feel this pull to go check, so I do. I haven't really broken it down like that before, to be honest. It's just become part of my routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that there might be something medically wrong with your hands or face, like a disease or a defect?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's a medical problem. I've never worried about it being a disease or anything like that. I just... I just don't like how they look. It's not a health issue, just an appearance thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hands and face are actually flawed in some way, or are you more concerned about what other people might think of how you look?\n\nPatient: Well, I really do think they're flawed. It's actually why I came in. I recently started helping out with the safety trainings at the plant, and... I just can't seem to stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look when I'm up there pointing things out to people. \n\nAnd then with my face, whenever I see myself in the company newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked. Other people tell me they don't see it at all, but I definitely do. So, yeah, I do think there's actually something wrong with how they look, even if other people say they don't notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be bothered by the appearance of your hands and face if you were alone and nobody else could see you?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest. I haven't thought about it like that. \n\nI mean, I mostly get bothered when I'm up doing those safety trainings or looking at the company newsletter. If I was just home alone and knew I didn't have to see anyone... I don't know. I still don't like how they look when I'm looking in my own mirror at the house, but I couldn't say for sure if it would weigh on me the exact same way. I just don't have a good answer for that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about the appearance of your hands and face if nobody else could see you?\n\nPatient: Like I just said, I really couldn't tell you. I haven't ever thought about it that way. I mean, I still see it when I look in the mirror at home, but I don't know if I'd care as much if I never had to go out and see people. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still think about your hands and face as much if you didn't have to interact with other people at work?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know how to answer that. I mean, it's just hard to say. The work trainings and the newsletters are what really set it off right now, but... if I didn't have to do those, or interact with people? I'm not sure if it would still be on my mind as much. I just haven't been in a situation like that to really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about how your hands and face look if you were in a situation where you knew nobody was judging you?\n\nPatient: I really don't know what else to tell you about that. I think it's just... well, it's really about how they look to me. I mean, yes, I worry about people noticing, like I said, but the main thing is that I see them and they just look wrong. Even if no one else cared, I'd still have to look at my own hands and face, and I'd still hate how they look. So, it's mostly about the actual way they look.

It's just really been bothering me, and I can't seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts about your hands and face affecting your work or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped me from doing my job or anything like that. I still go to work and I still do the safety trainings. But... I guess I am a bit slower getting ready for work on the days I have to do the trainings, just trying to make sure I look okay. And, like I said about the newsletter, I've definitely started ducking out of the way when I see a camera. I even find myself hesitating a bit before going to some of the work get-togethers or social things that I used to just go to without thinking about it. It just makes me feel a little more self-conscious, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, as a way to deal with these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. Before I have to do a safety class, or even if I'm just heading to a senior-center workshop, I’ll go check the bathroom mirror a few times. I'll put lotion on my hands, and I kind of turn my face different ways in the light just to see how it looks. \n\nSometimes I'll even ask a coworker if I look all right, just to be sure. Doing all that does make me run a little behind schedule sometimes, like I mentioned earlier, but... I still get myself there. I don't let it stop me from showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after checking your appearance in the mirror or getting reassurance from a coworker?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it like that. \n\nI guess I just feel like I need to check, but... I don't know if I actually feel any better or less anxious afterward. Even if a coworker says I look fine, I... I don't really know if it changes how I feel about it. I just sort of do it and then go get on with my shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror, or is it something you just do because you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't really say I want to do it. It's certainly not something I enjoy doing. But... I don't know if I'd say I absolutely have to, either. \n\nI guess it just feels like something I need to get out of the way before I go stand in front of people for a training. I've never worried about it being a disease or anything like that. I just... I just don't like how they look. It's not a health issue, just an appearance thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hands and face are actually flawed in some way, or are you more concerned about what other people might think of how you look?\n\nPatient: Well, I really do think they're flawed. They took some photos of the trainings for the company newsletter, and looking at them, I just keep thinking my face looks crooked. I've pointed it out, but other people say they don’t see it at all. It's just really been bothering me, and I can't seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts about your hands and face affecting your work or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped me from doing my job or anything like that. I still go to work and I still do the safety trainings. But... I guess I am a bit slower getting ready for work on the days I have to do the trainings, just trying to make sure I look okay. And, like I said about the newsletter, I've definitely started ducking out of the way when I see a camera. I even find myself hesitating a bit before going to some of the work get-togethers or social things that I used to just go to without thinking about it. It just makes me feel a little more self-conscious, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, as a way to deal with these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. Before I have to do a safety class, or even if I'm just heading to a senior-center workshop, I’ll go check the bathroom mirror a few times. I'll put lotion on my hands, and I kind of turn my face different ways in the light just to see how it looks. \n\nSometimes I'll even ask a coworker if I look all right, just to be sure. Doing all that does make me run a little behind schedule sometimes, like I mentioned earlier, but... I still get myself there. I don't let it stop me from showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after checking your appearance in the mirror or getting reassurance from a coworker?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it like that. \n\nI guess I just feel like I need to check, but... I don't know if I actually feel any better or less anxious afterward. Even if a coworker says I look fine, I... I don't really know if it changes how I feel about it. I just sort of do it and then go get on with my shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror, or is it something you just do because you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't really say I want to do it. It's certainly not something I enjoy doing. But... I don't know if I'd say I absolutely have to, either. \n\nI guess it just feels like something I need to get out of the way before I go stand in front of people for a training. I just feel this pull to go check, so I do. I haven't really broken it down like that before, to be honest. It's just become part of my routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that there might be something medically wrong with your hands or face, like a disease or a defect?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's a medical problem. I've never worried about it being a disease or anything like that. I just... I just don't like how they look. It's not a health issue, just an appearance thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hands and face are actually flawed in some way, or are you more concerned about what other people might think of how you look?\n\nPatient: Well, I really do think they're flawed. It's actually why I came in. I recently started helping out with the safety trainings at the plant, and... I just can't seem to stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look when I'm up there pointing things out to people. \n\nAnd then with my face, whenever I see myself in the company newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked. Other people tell me they don't see it at all, but I definitely do. So, yeah, I do think there's actually something wrong with how they look, even if other people say they don't notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be bothered by the appearance of your hands and face if you were alone and nobody else could see you?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest. I haven't thought about it like that. \n\nI mean, I mostly get bothered when I'm up doing those safety trainings or looking at the company newsletter. If I was just home alone and knew I didn't have to see anyone... I don't know. I still don't like how they look when I'm looking in my own mirror at the house, but I couldn't say for sure if it would weigh on me the exact same way. I just don't have a good answer for that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about the appearance of your hands and face if nobody else could see you?\n\nPatient: Like I just said, I really couldn't tell you. I haven't ever thought about it that way. I mean, I still see it when I look in the mirror at home, but I don't know if I'd care as much if I never had to go out and see people. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still think about your hands and face as much if you didn't have to interact with other people at work?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know how to answer that. I mean, it's just hard to say. The work trainings and the newsletters are what really set it off right now, but... if I didn't have to do those, or interact with people? I'm not sure if it would still be on my mind as much. I just haven't been in a situation like that to really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about how your hands and face look if you were in a situation where you knew nobody was judging you?\n\nPatient: I really don't know what else to tell you about that. I mean, it's just hard for me to imagine a situation where I wouldn't feel judged, you know? So I can't really say if I'd still be worried or not. I just... I don't know the answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face would decrease if you were certain that nobody was evaluating your appearance?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we're just going in circles with this one. Like I've been saying... I really don't know. I can't tell you if it would decrease because I just haven't ever been in a situation where I was absolutely certain no one was looking at me or evaluating me. I just don't have a different answer for you on that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance would go away if you were completely convinced that your hands and face looked normal?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, I guess if I really, truly believed they looked normal, then maybe I wouldn't worry about it as much. But the thing is, I don't think they look normal. So it's hard to say what I would feel if I saw something different when I looked in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance are more about how you feel about yourself or about what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure how to answer that. I mean, I guess it's a bit of both? I don't like how they look, and that makes me feel bad about myself. But then, because I think they look bad, I worry about what other people are thinking when they see me. I can't really separate the two, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance if you were certain that nobody would be looking at you or evaluating how you look?\n\nPatient: I... honestly, I just don't know. It's pretty much like what I was saying a minute ago. I can't really tell you if I'd still be checking the mirror or not, because I've just never been in a spot where I knew for an absolute fact that nobody would ever see me. I guess I might still check, but... I really just don't have a clear answer for you on that one either.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face are more about the actual way they look or about how you think others might react to them?\n\nPatient: I think it's just... well, it's really about how they look to me. I mean, yes, I worry about people noticing, like I said, but the main thing is that I see them and they just look wrong. Even if no one else cared, I'd still have to look at my own hands and face, and I'd still hate how they look. So, it's mostly about the actual way they look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance would decrease if you were in a private setting where you knew you would not be seen by anyone?\n\nPatient: Well... I'm really not sure. Like I was just saying, it's mostly about how they look to me. So even if I was completely alone in a room, I'd still be there, seeing my own hands and face. I don't know if the worrying would actually decrease or not... I've just never really thought about it that way to give you a straight answer.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It's just really been bothering me, and I can't seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts about your hands and face affecting your work or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped me from doing my job or anything like that. I still go to work and I still do the safety trainings. But... I guess I am a bit slower getting ready for work on the days I have to do the trainings, just trying to make sure I look okay. And, like I said about the newsletter, I've definitely started ducking out of the way when I see a camera. I even find myself hesitating a bit before going to some of the work get-togethers or social things that I used to just go to without thinking about it. It just makes me feel a little more self-conscious, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, as a way to deal with these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. Before I have to do a safety class, or even if I'm just heading to a senior-center workshop, I’ll go check the bathroom mirror a few times. I'll put lotion on my hands, and I kind of turn my face different ways in the light just to see how it looks. \n\nSometimes I'll even ask a coworker if I look all right, just to be sure. Doing all that does make me run a little behind schedule sometimes, like I mentioned earlier, but... I still get myself there. I don't let it stop me from showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after checking your appearance in the mirror or getting reassurance from a coworker?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it like that. \n\nI guess I just feel like I need to check, but... I don't know if I actually feel any better or less anxious afterward. Even if a coworker says I look fine, I... I don't really know if it changes how I feel about it. I just sort of do it and then go get on with my shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror, or is it something you just do because you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't really say I want to do it. It's certainly not something I enjoy doing. But... I don't know if I'd say I absolutely have to, either. \n\nI guess it just feels like something I need to get out of the way before I go stand in front of people for a training. I just feel this pull to go check, so I do. I haven't really broken it down like that before, to be honest. It's just become part of my routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that there might be something medically wrong with your hands or face, like a disease or a defect?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's a medical problem. I've never worried about it being a disease or anything like that. I just... I just don't like how they look. It's not a health issue, just an appearance thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hands and face are actually flawed in some way, or are you more concerned about what other people might think of how you look?\n\nPatient: Well, I really do think they're flawed. It's actually why I came in. I recently started helping out with the safety trainings at the plant, and... I just can't seem to stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look when I'm up there pointing things out to people. \n\nAnd then with my face, whenever I see myself in the company newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked. Other people tell me they don't see it at all, but I definitely do. So, yeah, I do think there's actually something wrong with how they look, even if other people say they don't notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be bothered by the appearance of your hands and face if you were alone and nobody else could see you?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest. I haven't thought about it like that. \n\nI mean, I mostly get bothered when I'm up doing those safety trainings or looking at the company newsletter. If I was just home alone and knew I didn't have to see anyone... I don't know. I still don't like how they look when I'm looking in my own mirror at the house, but I couldn't say for sure if it would weigh on me the exact same way. I just don't have a good answer for that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about the appearance of your hands and face if nobody else could see you?\n\nPatient: Like I just said, I really couldn't tell you. I haven't ever thought about it that way. I mean, I still see it when I look in the mirror at home, but I don't know if I'd care as much if I never had to go out and see people. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still think about your hands and face as much if you didn't have to interact with other people at work?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know how to answer that. I mean, it's just hard to say. The work trainings and the newsletters are what really set it off right now, but... if I didn't have to do those, or interact with people? I'm not sure if it would still be on my mind as much. I just haven't been in a situation like that to really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about how your hands and face look if you were in a situation where you knew nobody was judging you?\n\nPatient: I really don't know what else to tell you about that. I mean, it's just hard for me to imagine a situation where I wouldn't feel judged, you know? So I can't really say if I'd still be worried or not. I just... I don't know the answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face would decrease if you were certain that nobody was evaluating your appearance?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we're just going in circles with this one. Like I've been saying... I really don't know. I can't tell you if it would decrease because I just haven't ever been in a situation where I was absolutely certain no one was looking at me or evaluating me. I just don't have a different answer for you on that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance would go away if you were completely convinced that your hands and face looked normal?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, I guess if I really, truly believed they looked normal, then maybe I wouldn't worry about it as much. But the thing is, I don't think they look normal. So it's hard to say what I would feel if I saw something different when I looked in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance are more about how you feel about yourself or about what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure how to answer that. I mean, I guess it's a bit of both? I don't like how they look, and that makes me feel bad about myself. But then, because I think they look bad, I worry about what other people are thinking when they see me. I can't really separate the two, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance if you were certain that nobody would be looking at you or evaluating how you look?\n\nPatient: I... honestly, I just don't know. It's pretty much like what I was saying a minute ago. I can't really tell you if I'd still be checking the mirror or not, because I've just never been in a spot where I knew for an absolute fact that nobody would ever see me. I guess I might still check, but... I really just don't have a clear answer for you on that one either.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face are more about the actual way they look or about how you think others might react to them?\n\nPatient: I think it's just... well, it's really about how they look to me. I mean, yes, I worry about people noticing, like I said, but the main thing is that I see them and they just look wrong. Even if no one else cared, I'd still have to look at my own hands and face, and I'd still hate how they look. So, it's mostly about the actual way they look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance would decrease if you were in a private setting where you knew you would not be seen by anyone?\n\nPatient: Well... I'm really not sure. Like I was just saying, it's mostly about how they look to me. So even if I was completely alone in a room, I'd still be there, seeing my own hands and face. I don't know if the worrying would actually decrease or not... I've just never really thought about it that way to give you a straight answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still perceive your hands and face as flawed if you never received any feedback about your appearance from others?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I said, it's really about how they look to me, so I think I'd still see them the same way even if nobody ever said a word to me about it. But I've never been in a situation where I didn't get any kind of reaction or look from people, so I can't say for sure. I just... I don't really have a good answer for that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So if they don't even see it, but it still bothers me this much... I guess I'd still see them as flawed even if I was the only person looking.

They took some photos of the trainings for the company newsletter, and looking at them, I just keep thinking my face looks crooked. I've pointed it out, but other people say they don’t see it at all. It's just really been bothering me, and I can't seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts about your hands and face affecting your work or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped me from doing my job or anything like that. I still go to work and I still do the safety trainings. But... I guess I am a bit slower getting ready for work on the days I have to do the trainings, just trying to make sure I look okay. And, like I said about the newsletter, I've definitely started ducking out of the way when I see a camera. I even find myself hesitating a bit before going to some of the work get-togethers or social things that I used to just go to without thinking about it. It just makes me feel a little more self-conscious, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, as a way to deal with these thoughts about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. Before I have to do a safety class, or even if I'm just heading to a senior-center workshop, I’ll go check the bathroom mirror a few times. I'll put lotion on my hands, and I kind of turn my face different ways in the light just to see how it looks. \n\nSometimes I'll even ask a coworker if I look all right, just to be sure. Doing all that does make me run a little behind schedule sometimes, like I mentioned earlier, but... I still get myself there. I don't let it stop me from showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after checking your appearance in the mirror or getting reassurance from a coworker?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it like that. \n\nI guess I just feel like I need to check, but... I don't know if I actually feel any better or less anxious afterward. Even if a coworker says I look fine, I... I don't really know if it changes how I feel about it. I just sort of do it and then go get on with my shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror, or is it something you just do because you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't really say I want to do it. It's certainly not something I enjoy doing. But... I don't know if I'd say I absolutely have to, either. \n\nI guess it just feels like something I need to get out of the way before I go stand in front of people for a training. It's not a health issue, just an appearance thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hands and face are actually flawed in some way, or are you more concerned about what other people might think of how you look?\n\nPatient: Well, I really do think they're flawed. It's actually why I came in. I recently started helping out with the safety trainings at the plant, and... Other people tell me they don't see it at all, but I definitely do. So, yeah, I do think there's actually something wrong with how they look, even if other people say they don't notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be bothered by the appearance of your hands and face if you were alone and nobody else could see you?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest. I haven't thought about it like that. \n\nI mean, I mostly get bothered when I'm up doing those safety trainings or looking at the company newsletter. If I was just home alone and knew I didn't have to see anyone... I don't know. I still don't like how they look when I'm looking in my own mirror at the house, but I couldn't say for sure if it would weigh on me the exact same way. I just don't have a good answer for that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about the appearance of your hands and face if nobody else could see you?\n\nPatient: Like I just said, I really couldn't tell you. I haven't ever thought about it that way. I mean, I still see it when I look in the mirror at home, but I don't know if I'd care as much if I never had to go out and see people. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still think about your hands and face as much if you didn't have to interact with other people at work?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know how to answer that. I mean, it's just hard to say. The work trainings and the newsletters are what really set it off right now, but... if I didn't have to do those, or interact with people? I'm not sure if it would still be on my mind as much. I just haven't been in a situation like that to really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about how your hands and face look if you were in a situation where you knew nobody was judging you?\n\nPatient: I really don't know what else to tell you about that. I just don't have a different answer for you on that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance would go away if you were completely convinced that your hands and face looked normal?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, I guess if I really, truly believed they looked normal, then maybe I wouldn't worry about it as much. But the thing is, I don't think they look normal. So it's hard to say what I would feel if I saw something different when I looked in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance are more about how you feel about yourself or about what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure how to answer that. I guess I'd still see them as flawed even if I was the only person looking.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your concerns about your hands and face would still exist if you were completely alone and had no possibility of social interaction?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / production_worker / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like that's pretty similar to what we were just talking about, and... I honestly just don't know. If I was completely alone forever, with no social interaction at all... I mean, I'd still have my own eyes. I'd still see my hands when I'm doing things, and I'd still see my face in the mirror. So I imagine it would probably still bother me. 

But since I've never been in a situation like that, living totally cut off from the world, it's just hard to say for sure. I really can't give you a definite answer on that one.

I'm not sure if it would still be on my mind as much.
